Advice from Sam Altman’s coach that’ll piss off every hustle culture bro

My First Million(955.0K)·86:06·16.9K views·2026-09-18

counter-intuitive emotional...

TL;DR

High-performance coach Joe Hudson breaks down the mental, emotional, and nervous system patterns of elite tech founders with hosts Shaan Puri and Sam Parr, explaining how to untangle self-reliance, conflict avoidance, and negative self-talk through counter-intuitive emotional acceptance and weekly experimentation.

Mind, Heart, and Nervous System: The Tri-Level Framework

  • The Three Levels of Transformation: Real personal or professional change requires addressing three distinct systems rather than just intellectual understanding:
    • The Mind (Prefrontal Cortex): The easiest level for high-achievers to grasp intellectually, though knowing what to do rarely translates to doing it.
    • The Heart (Mammalian/Emotional): The emotional center where underlying fears and avoided feelings must be processed.
    • The Nervous System (Reptilian): The primal, physical layer (triggering a physical startle response to a Slack message or a late arrival) which changes not through a-has, but via repetition and expanding baseline pleasure.
  • The Self-Reliance Pattern: Traced back to childhood where individuals learned they had to fend for themselves and others (e.g., dealing with critical or addicted parents), creating adults who:
    • Struggle to ask for help, own their desires, or announce when they are hurt.
    • Harbor a complicated relationship with authority—both rebelling against external authorities and refusing to fully step into their own.
  • The Golden Algorithm of Emotion: The emotion you are trying to avoid is the exact emotion you end up inviting into your life by the way you try to avoid it.
    • *Example:* Avoiding the feeling of helplessness by over-grinding leads to burnout, poor decisions, and ultimately, a deeper state of helplessness.
    • *Solution:* Fall in love with the emotions you are avoiding; treat them as messengers and signals rather than enemies.

Rewiring Self-Talk and Negative Thought Loops

  • Abusive Self-Talk Patterns: High-achievers are frequently their own worst abusers, trapped in loops of overt criticism ("Why did you do that?"), endless pressure ("You've got to do this now"), or subtle undermining ("You can do it, Mike").
  • Reaction Over Content: Trying to change the self-talk directly is inefficient; instead, change your *reaction* to the self-talk by treating it playfully or as bad management (e.g., responding to an internal critique with, "Oh, I see how scared you are. Prove it.").
  • Debunking the Narrative: List the top 20 things the negative voice in your head says and find at least 7 concrete ways each is false (or ask friends to help prove it wrong).

The Psychology of Founders and Conflict Avoidance

  • Conflict as Progress: Avoiding conflict in a marriage or business is equivalent to ignoring a blinking warning light on a factory machine; conflict is the exact mechanism through which efficiency and alignment are gained.
  • Organizational Health Metrics: Joe Hudson evaluates corporate health using just two metrics: how quickly an organization has hard conversations, and how quickly it resolves them.
  • Conducting Hard Conversations:
    • Do not solve the problem for the person (which is just a manifestation of CEO self-reliance); instead, ask them to present a plan to solve it by tomorrow.
    • Prioritize understanding their context via open-ended questions before delivering feedback.
    • Express your own vulnerability (e.g., "I feel like we've lost trust and that sucks for me") rather than making it entirely about attacking the other person.
  • The Psyche of Top 1% Performers:
    • Exceptional fast-paced CEOs rarely second-guess themselves because the velocity of decision-making demands decisiveness.
    • Approximately 60% to 70% of high performers harbor an underlying paradox: they simultaneously feel superior to everyone else (conscious mind) and deeply broken (underneath), using achievement to prove they are not broken.

Practical Weekly Experiments for Growth

  • The 10% Enjoyment Experiment: Take what you are already doing and find a way to enjoy it 5% to 10% more without changing your actual actions, doing this roughly 5 times a day for a week.
  • The Do-Only-What-You-Enjoy Week: Commit to doing only what you enjoy for a week; when forced to confront an unenviable task (like taking out the trash), figure out how to genuinely enjoy the process rather than enduring it.
  • The Gratitude Shift: Consciously look around every day and thank 5 people for what they did for you to actively dismantle the self-reliance pattern.
  • Visualization of the Worst-Case Scenario: Like samurai or stoics visualizing their own death or the destruction of their company, thoroughly feel the worst-case outcomes (such as homelessness or corporate collapse) so that fear no longer dictates your business decisions.

Notable Quotes

*"The emotion that you're trying to avoid is the emotion you're inviting in the exact way that you're trying to avoid it."* — Joe Hudson
*"If you want to play on like... 'What you seek you shall find.' It's almost like what you avoid, you shall experience."* — Sam Parr
*"Meditation for most people is just self-abuse, because they're just sitting there trying to manage themselves for 90 minutes... The question is, how do you enjoy meditation?"* — Joe Hudson

And you're like, they're quitting? I feel like you get goosebumps and you feel something. How do you address this at a neurological level? Yeah, so nervous system, that's a super tricky one. There's a lot of things that you can do. But this is all very primal and this is where repetition and, "Do I do this every day?" really comes in handy. There's not an intellectual or emotional aha that can change a nervous system just like that. Occasionally, high doses of psilocybin or something, I've seen change nervous systems. But that's very undependable. And so this is actually where repetition matters in the nervous system. One of the key principles is how do you allow yourself to experience pleasure more and more deeply? Because pleasure means that we're not under attack. So our nervous system thinks that we're under attack when we're not. And so it amps. Now, sometimes that occurs because we have an abusive self-talk. If I was your boss, and I was like, "Oh you fucked that up, you did that wrong. Why are you biting your nails? Why aren't you looking at Joe while you're podcasting? Why are you taking notes instead of being connected to your co-host?" If I was doing that perpetually, it would cause stress. Because you're perpetually under attack. But often we're doing that to ourselves, and so that's activating the nervous system. So that is the intellectual, that's how the intellect and the nervous system relate. But there's a whole bunch of things that also happen with the nervous system, and I'll use my own example in this. My mom, German, super rigid woman, loved me dearly in her capacity, but if I was five minutes early, I was late and I would get in trouble. It was that kind of a thing and things needed to be very perfect. And so, there was a sense of obligation. And if I wasn't going to fulfill that obligation, I was going to be in trouble. And so, my nervous system gets activated when I'm a minute late for a podcast. Though it's not going to affect anything, my nervous system's going to get messed up. So, there's some nervous system stuff that happens, just through training. So, one of the overall background thought processes is, how do I allow as much pleasure as possible to reset my baseline? But the second thing is to really start to notice and be present with for like one or two seconds, those moments when my nervous system is activated, or when your nervous system is activated. So, when that phone call comes in and you're like, oh shit, you just stop, and you're like, what is it like to be me? Two seconds, and then you go ahead and do your thing. Like, go ahead, be anxious again. Don't try to control yourself because trying to control your anxiety is just anxiety producing. It just creates more of the same thing. It's just being really in touch with what's going on, bringing awareness into that moment, is all that's really necessary. But it's necessary to do it over and over and over again. To see what's actually occurring in your system and what's really necessary. And then pretty soon, it unpacks what's going on beneath your nervous system, your intellect starts to understand it. Typically, if I'm talking about it, let's just take one example, because this gets a little complex. If I want to have a hard conversation with somebody, what do I do? The first thing that I'm going to do is I am going to not solve the problem for them. To most people in a hard conversation, I say, "Hey, sales are off, and what I need you to do is make more phone calls, and we're going to monitor those through this set of data." That's incredibly disempowering, and that conversation usually doesn't go very well. But if you say instead, "Hey, sales are off, I need a plan, please present it to me tomorrow about how we're going to get sales back on track," that's a far more powerful way to do it. Because you're not disempowering the person, and you're not coming in and telling them what to do. Letting them solve the problem for you rather than you solve the problem for them. You solving the problem for them is self-reliance again, and it's what most CEOs do, instead of letting them solve the problem for you, which is far more empowering to them, and it makes them feel like they can't blame you for it. So, that's one of the skills that I would teach. The other one is, I would really try to understand their situation before having the hard conversation. I would ask a lot of open-ended questions. I would make sure that they feel very heard. As the CEO, I sit down and I'm like, "I have an idea of what's going on over here in billing, but I don't. I do not have the full picture. If I have the full picture, I should fire the person, because they're not doing a job good enough where I don't have to pay attention." So, the first thing I want to do is really ask their context and understand what's actually going on with them and see if they're even happy with the job that they're doing, or what's going on actually. Another thing I would do is I would actually be very vulnerable about where I am with it. I would say something like, "Wow, I feel like we've lost trust. I know that I'm not trusting that this is going to get done, and so I'm not having confidence around this, and that sucks for me, and that's got to suck for you. And so I want to know how we're going to fix it." I'm going to be very vulnerable with how I am, rather than making it about them. This is again back to the self-reliance piece. Entrepreneurs who are self-reliant have a hard time expressing when they're hurt. They have a hard time expressing some sort of "This isn't working for me" really well, and therefore the problem is always about the other person, instead of about them helping fix your issues, which is the way an organization is designed. The energy is backwards if you're trying to fix everybody's issues rather than everybody's fixing your issues. If I was to invest in your company, as an example, and I got to pick one person who was just stoked as hell, I would pick the CEO. Because that's going to be the best investment, the CEO is happy with the way the company is going, the company is going the way it should be going. If the head of marketing is happy, but the CEO is miserable about the way the company is going, that's not a good investment. So, the happiness of the CEO is where everybody needs to be pointed. It doesn't mean that the CEO's job isn't to be helpful to people, but it means that the general energy of the company is supposed to be towards pleasing the CEO, making the CEO happy. Which self-reliant CEOs have a really hard time getting through their head, because that puts them in the position of the authority that they don't particularly want to be because authority was bad in their childhood. Are there any patterns that you have seen of self-talk, and what are the practical tips on improving that? There's about three or four different kind of self-talk abuse cycles. There's like the really overt one, which is like, "Why are you—why didn't you, why don't you do this, why don't you do that?" There's that version. Then there's the version of, "You got to do this, now you got to do this, you got to do this, why don't you do this, you got to do this, don't forget to do this, you got to do this." There's another version of negative self-talk. There's some kind of hidden ones which are like, "You've got it. You can do this. It's all good, you've got this thing. You can do this thing," which you would—could you imagine going to Michael Jordan and going, "Yeah, you can do it. You could shoot the basket. I know you can, Mike." You would never do it, because you have confidence in that person. So this is a subtle undermining, saying that you actually need this. It's not, you're fine either way, and there's not a natural confidence in it. There are about 20 tools to work with negative self-talk. One of the ways that you know it's negative self-talk is because it's repeating. When negative self-talk starts dying in your system, when it starts atrophying, you just have a lot less thought. Just a lot less thought occurs. And the thought that you have is more like epiphanies. It's like, "Oh, here's a cool idea. Oh, that's a cool idea." They're not repetitive, over and over again thoughts. I'll give you just a couple really practical ones. If you try to change the self-talk, it's very inefficient. If you change the way you react to the self-talk, it's far more efficient. Your self-talk is like, "Wow, you just really screwed up that podcast, Joe." Most people listen to that the way that they would listen to—think about the politician you hate most talking on stage, and then think about the person in the audience who's like, "Yep, uh-huh, yeah, that's right, uh-huh." That's how we listen to our self-talk. Most of us listen to our self-talk as, "Oh, you screwed that up." And it's like, "Yep, I screwed that up, I should do something about that." That's the relationship. So instead, you change the reaction to it. Like, "Oh, you screwed that stuff up." "Oh, I see how scared you are. I'm right here with you, you can be scared." Or, "You fucked up." "Wow, that's really shitty management. Could you try to manage me in a different way, please?" You're changing the reaction and so, a great experiment to run on this is think about the reaction that you want to experiment with. It doesn't have to be good or bad, it's just an experiment. Every time your negative self-talk happens that day, every time you remember, you react in this way that you're experimenting with. And then two days later, you do a new experiment. You're constantly changing the way that you're reacting to your self-talk. And just like both of you are different when you're interacting with each other than when you're interacting with your significant other, or your kids if you have them, or your best friend from college, the voice in our head reacts differently depending on how we treat it, and you get to start seeing that. That's a really simple way to start managing, not managing, but start bringing awareness and therefore changing and transforming your negative self-talk. So if I had a car, if I wanted an efficient company, we think of it as a fast company—I got from A to B quickly. But if we had a car, "A to B quickly" isn't an efficient car; that's just a fast car. I like talking with people like you because you have this really cool perspective where you've talked to the Titans of industry, the best of the best, the Illuminati, you know? You have seen them. And we all know what they appear like in the media or on podcasts like this where they usually look like they have their act together, or they'll show just a little bit of vulnerability to show that they're one of us. Yeah, specifically I can't do, obviously, but what I could say is that there's nothing I've said on this podcast that I haven't said to one of the Titans of industry. Like, the things that you're wrestling with are the exact same things that they're wrestling with, right. What attributes do you tend to notice? Are they 10 out of 10 on, or 1 out of 10 on? The really, really exceptional ones of, like, very fast-paced businesses—so not like if you can be a slow-paced business, then there's a different kind of attitude, you can actually be quite self-abusive in a slow-paced business—but in a fast-paced business, you can't second-guess yourself very much. So, very, very few of them are second-guessers. They just are. Part of it is because they have to make so many decisions in a day. And so, decisiveness is another one, especially for the fast-paced industries. There are some, like investment—if you're an investor and you can be a billionaire investor, you can really second-guess yourself a ton and still be a successful investor. But if you're the CEO of a tech company that's in a fast-paced market, you can't do that. The other thing that I think those very high performers—it's not all of them, but I would say at least 60, maybe 70%—they both feel like they're better than everybody and worse than everybody simultaneously. So they, on one level, in their conscious mind, are like, "I am more capable than the average person," but underneath they're like, "I am broken." And so, they're kind of doing this thing because they know that they're better and they're trying to prove that they're not broken. It's not all the time, but it's more common in that population than in any other population that I've seen. There's a lot of people who walk around thinking that they're better than everybody, right. I mean, you can go down into the dregs of our society and you will find somebody who thinks they're better than everybody, but that underlying "oh I'm broken" simultaneously with it, and the way that that presents, is very unique. There's a lot of it in that realm. You know, one of the things we did in this podcast that's sort of backwards probably from most podcasts—most podcasts start with "tell me about your background and your story"—and we do it the opposite. When I've read something about like an eight-second meditation experience, I'm very curious about that. But if you give us the journey, make sure you hit that along the way. Okay. Yeah, I mean, I grew up going all over the world. I think by the time I was 28, I'd lived in 26 places. My dad was a CEO but also became an alcoholic, and so I rebelled against that—had a mohawk, was a skateboarder, got kicked out of my first college, graduated valedictorian from my second college. And then, first thing I did when I left college was like, "I'm just having adventures one after the other." Like James Bond, man. Well, it's 'cause I saw my dad just do this corporate thing and end up in misery. I was like, "Yeah, I'm not doing that." Then I met my wife, and my wife was like, "If we're going to get married, we're going to see a therapist, we're going to do a 10-day silent retreat, and we're going to travel through the third world together for like six months. If we can do those three things, we're going to be capable of getting married and being successful." So those were her tasks, and so I did them. The therapy was amazing; we got blessed with a really good therapist, and they're pretty rare. And then the silent retreat was my first time meditating ever. And on day three of the Vipassana, when it changes from putting all your attention into your breath to moving your attention through your body, I had this experience of, like, evaporation. I—samadhi is what some people would call it, but this experience of, "I am one with everything and everything is good." And it lasted eight seconds, and it was like getting hooked on heroin. My entire life changed. I was like, "Whatever the hell that is, I'm obsessed." Unfortunately, I went about it in a very inefficient way. I very much was like, "Once I get there, I'll be happy." It was just the search for enlightenment was just like porn for perfectionism. But the plus side was that, because I had authority issues, I just went and explored everything, took nothing very seriously, and made everything an experiment. So now, that's how we teach—when we do our courses, everything is done as an experiment. It's not like we're talking and saying this is how things are; we're like, "Go and experience the shit for yourself." Here's how you do it, you can do it with people right now. It's very in-the-moment experimentation. And I wasn't willing to say, "You're the authority, you're the guru." I was like, "I'm going to listen to every single modality that I can find, from neuroscience to psychology to traditions that are 2,000 years old. I'm going to try 10 forms of meditation, I'm going to do the Hoffman process, I'm going to do somatic work." I just did everything, and I was totally dedicated to that. I didn't work for seven years; I lived off credit cards, I meditated for like seven hours a day, searching. And I was obsessed with it. The search ended, what people would call "Awakening" happened, and it wasn't an end at all. There was no finish line. In doing venture, the thing that I realized about the personal work was that if you take a standard business tool and you put the awareness of personal work into it, the standard business tool gets better. Right? If we have something that we teach called "view," it's in our connection course. It's about how to communicate with people, how to have hard conversations, how to have sales conversations. When people have it, they sell better, they do product better, they talk to their husbands and wives better, they have hard conversations better—it's because they're all related. Bending Spoons is Eating Silicon Valley | CEO Luca Ferrari

Sourcery with Molly O'Shea(72.3K)·74:57·3.5K views·2026-09-18
Luca FerrariCo-Founder & CEO, Bending Spoons Molly O'SheaHost, Sourcery
TL;DR- **Target Criteria:** Bending Spoons targets digital businesses, consumer internet, SaaS, and selectively hardware (like the recent pet-tracking acquisition Tractive in spring), ensuring they can predict trajectories multiple years into the future.
Bending Spoons CEO Luca Ferrari explains how the Milan-based tech company acquires digital businesses, integrates them onto a proprietary 50+ tool operating system, and achieves massive efficiency—including 95% of code written by AI and $4 million in revenue per core team...

TL;DR: Bending Spoons CEO Luca Ferrari explains how the Milan-based tech company acquires digital businesses, integrates them onto a proprietary 50+ tool operating system, and achieves massive efficiency—including 95% of code written by AI and $4 million in revenue per core team member.

Acquisition Strategy and Philosophy

  • Target Criteria: Bending Spoons targets digital businesses, consumer internet, SaaS, and selectively hardware (like the recent pet-tracking acquisition Tractive in spring), ensuring they can predict trajectories multiple years into the future.
  • Active Value Creation: Unlike passive private equity funds that buy-to-hold or hands-off financial operators, Bending Spoons deeply transforms acquired companies through product, tech, organizational, and monetization overhauls.
  • The Airtable Acquisition:
    • Deal Details: Acquired at an enterprise value of approximately $1.3 billion, with cash remaining on the balance sheet so investors recouped their capital plus more.
    • Silicon Valley Reaction: Caused industry hysteria because Airtable was seen as a golden child, but Ferrari argues the exit valuation aligned with comparable public SaaS multiples and represents a massive success.

Centralized Technology Platform & AI Operations

  • Shared Operating System: Built over the past decade, the platform encompasses over 50 proprietary technologies handling data storage, AB testing, payments, recruiting, credentials, and AI model orchestration.
  • Financial Impact: Building these tools in-house saves Bending Spoons at least $100 million a year in costs.
  • AI Code Generation: Approximately 95% of code across Bending Spoons is written by AI.
  • Alt-Spooner Agents:
    • Integration: Internal Slack agents tied to the user's exact permissions across all company platforms.
    • Capabilities: Can perform data analysis (e.g., pulling AB test results in 5 minutes) or execute end-to-end engineering tasks (e.g., an Evernote GM having her agent find a customer support bug, check codebases, write a fix, and ping an engineering lead).
  • Model Orchestration & Vendor Independence:
    • Cost Control: An internal orchestration algorithm automatically routes requests to the most appropriate model, using self-hosted open-weight models for 99% of requests (virtually free) and frontier closed-weight models via APIs for only 1% of complex tasks or supervision.
    • Independence: Avoids vendor lock-in and protects against massive price increases.

Talent and Extreme Culture

  • Application-to-Hire Ratio: In 2025, Bending Spoons received 800,000 job applications and made fewer than 300 hires.
  • Revenue Efficiency: Core team ("Spooners") approaches 1,000 people, generating approximately $4 million in revenue per core employee (up from about $1 million 2–3 years ago); total headcount including acquired teams is over 2,000.
  • Core Cultural Pillars:
    • Extreme Ownership: Valuing deep care and commitment to team and company success over raw intelligence alone.
    • Scientific Truth-Seeking: Operating with extreme rationality and intellectual humility; the company ran over 3,000 quantitative experiments across its products last year.
  • Employee Retention: Core team unwanted churn is exceptionally low at 0.6% annually, driven by the ability to rotate across different products and internal platform challenges.

Misconceptions vs. Reality

  • Graveyard Myth: Dismisses the notion that acquired brands are " graveyard" companies, noting they serve half a billion monthly active users (e.g., AOL remains the 5th most used email provider in the Western world with tens of millions of active users).
  • Private Equity Distinction: Emphasizes that Bending Spoons is not a PE fund (they do not raise money from limited partners to sell companies in 5 years; they buy to own and operate forever).

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Growth Trajectory: Luca Ferrari states that Bending Spoons sees "no end in sight" and no obvious saturation point for how big their acquisitions and company growth can get.
  • AI Capability Gap: Luca Ferrari predicts that the gap in AI capabilities will continue to grow both vertically (doing specific tasks much better) and horizontally (taking on an increasing percentage of tasks better than humans), noting short of a world war, there is no stopping this trajectory.

Notable Quotes

  • > "We don't care too much about being cool, I would say we probably don't care at all about being cool, we care about being good at our jobs and, and creating value." — Luca Ferrari
  • > "We have an algorithm that will select the most appropriate AI model, or multiple AI models to get the job done, considering say, quality, effectiveness, but also cost. And it can draw from many, many different models. We end up using open weight models we self-host. So, these are basically free." — Luca Ferrari
  • > "I am, equal parts enthusiastic about AI, and absolutely scared shitless, I think this will be, this could be, you could definitely make a case for this being the greatest boon for humanity ever by orders of magnitude... equally it could be the thing that wipes us out or creates equally awful scenarios." — Luca Ferrari
We have half a billion monthly active users. I mean that's a lot, we've built over the past decade an operating system of over 50 proprietary technologies, we buy these companies and then we install them on this shared operating system, at Bending Spoons we've been able to attract, I think extremely strong talent, we had 800,000 job applications in 2025, we hired fewer than 300 people. Luca Ferrari, thank you for having me at Bending Spoons. Well, thank you for coming. We're all the way out in Milan, Italy. Yeah, I guess, I mean Airtable is a, is a great business, a great product and, and, and you can say of Airtable, something you can't say of a lot of different businesses, which, which is that they, they really were identified as a high-flying, you know, key company in, in Silicon Valley for, for quite a while, and that's very difficult to do, so you know, great job, Howie and everybody else who built that business, and so I think once the announcement of the acquisition came, that was a lot more newsworthy and interesting than maybe some other acquisitions we have done before. So, how did you pick that one? What was the process? Well, you pick each other when it comes to acquisitions, because it's something that has to be bidirectional, but we look at a lot of companies. I'd say actively, we may look at hundreds of companies in any one year and then we try to establish a dialogue with the dozens where we see the best match, and if some of these are interested in selling, then the conversation can progress and we look for businesses where we believe we can bring a lot of value, we are very active acquirer, and so for us, for us to be absolutely confident we can offer a key cast price while delivering strong returns for our shareholders, we need to be able to bring a lot of value, whether it's by improving the product or the technology or monetization, the organization, ideally all of these, so that's a key criterion, then we look for businesses that we believe we, whose trajectory we can predict with confidence, multiple years into the future, otherwise it's difficult to underwrite big investments and, and, and it's typically been digital businesses, so we've done consumer Internet, SaaS, these are the key, key areas for us, we also did, recently we acquired a hardware company called Tracktive in spring, which is a very interesting, path tracking and, and, and also help monitoring for pets, and the pet industry is booming, so that was a very nice acquisition, a little bit outside of our usual comfort zone. Because the Airtable acquisition kind of set SF, Silicon Valley, American Tech into a bit of hysteria, what do you think they get wrong about technology companies or managing them? I mean not a lot clearly. Silicon Valley has built, I know 70% of the most successful technology businesses of the past 25 to 50 years. So, I think Silicon Valley gets almost everything right. I believe we have been able to do well acquiring a tiny fraction of the businesses that have come out of Silicon Valley because we bring something different and new to the table in both the way we're structured and, and the platform we've built. That's for the most part unavailable to these businesses on a stand-alone basis, so for people who don't know Bending Spoons very well, unlike most serial acquirers out there, most, most serial acquirers out there will either buy a business because they think it's good as it is, and they can't really improve it, but the price is low enough that they can get good returns, and then they'll basically leave it alone, and that model can work. I don't think it will ever give you exceptional returns, but it can deliver reliable, potentially appealing returns if you're very good at picking businesses that are slightly undervalued. Others are more active, but they will still keep these businesses separate as they were before, but they, these acquires may have an opinion on how to price the product or how to build the organs, so they will go in and make changes. We are even more extreme on the, being active end of the spectrum, and we integrate all of these businesses together quite tightly, so we, we have built over the past decade an operating system of over 50 proprietary technologies to take care of almost everything you need to run a digital business, whether it's data storage and processing, AB testing, payment management, the, you know, everything related to recruiting, and, and, and credentials management, the, the orchestration for all the AI models used in operations, and so on and so forth. And so we, we buy these companies and then we, it's almost like we, we install them on this shared operating system, so they can be much more efficient, and also we have a core team for R&D, marketing, at this point approaching a thousand people, we, we can deploy very fluidly and rapidly across these various businesses to after R&D opportunities and when the R&D opportunities are not as exciting any longer, we can, take out the talent and move it elsewhere, so we stay very efficient, so these are aspects that, I believe none of these, none of the teams running these companies on a stand-alone basis really could access, and, and so it's not a lot of the value we create is because other people are, are myopic, they're doing the best they can with the resources available to them. But, but I do think we bring also something extra in terms of culture, we have developed a culture of extreme rationality, almost a scientific approach to running businesses, whereby, we are not afraid of making unpopular choices when we believe it's for the benefit of the business in the long run, so we tend to run these businesses very leanly, using data extensively, sometimes we, we joke about taking more established companies and bringing them back to startup mode, small team, very talent-dense teams, removing red tape, giving these people plenty of, of room to maneuver to experiment, to, to move fast, and we have found that that generally delivers a lot of value for customers and for the business. So, I think one of the big misconceptions with the types of companies that you acquire is that they're graveyard kind of companies, they're old brands, they're not new, maybe they're distressed assets, that kind of thing, what is wrong about that? Oh, I think it's just, that, I think people just try to frame things in a way that will get clicks, but I'll give you a stat. We have half a billion monthly active users, I mean that's a lot, short of being, you know, Google or Meta, how many companies have, so, if half a billion people using these products every month is a graveyard, then sure, let's call it that. Databricks CEO: Stop Scaring People About AI

a16z(296.0K)·66:53·15.1K views·2026-09-18
Ali GhodsiCo-Founder & CEO, Databricks Martin CasadoGeneral Partner, a16z Sarah WangGeneral Partner, a16z
TL;DRAli Ghodsi argues that AI existential risk is close to zero and public panic is counterproductive, though immediate dangers like automated cyberattacks require rapid defensive adoption.
Instead of waiting for frontier models, enterprises can unlock massive productivity today by...

TL;DR

Ali Ghodsi argues that AI existential risk is close to zero and public panic is counterproductive, though immediate dangers like automated cyberattacks require rapid defensive adoption. Instead of waiting for frontier models, enterprises can unlock massive productivity today by structuring institutional data into an organizational "ontology" to give AI necessary context.

AI Risk, Safety, and the Frontier Debate

  • Existential Risk Realism: Ali Ghodsi states that current existential risk is close to zero and that leaders should avoid unnecessarily terrifying the public, noting that his sister in rural Arizona texted him asking if she should prep her cabin for the AI apocalypse.
  • The Pacing vs. Security Branding Flaw: Martin Casado and Ali Ghodsi debate industry messaging, agreeing that framing safety around "pacing" the frontier rather than "securing" it alienates both doomers and policymakers while confusing the public.
  • The Four Criteria for Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI): Ali Ghodsi outlines that true RSI leading toward superintelligence would require four simultaneous conditions: next-generation models requiring super-linearly fewer resources, shorter training times, increasing accuracy/intelligence, and the ability to repeat this loop endlessly. He notes the industry is currently experiencing the exact opposite (more brittle, longer, more expensive training runs).
  • The Cyber Threat Reality: Both speakers agree that while superintelligence is far away, the immediate and severe risk is cybersecurity, with the time from a CVE vulnerability being published to weaponized exploits shrinking from years (in 2018–2019) to mere hours today.

Enterprise Adoption and the Context Gap

  • The Context Deficit: Ali Ghodsi explains that models are already smart enough to automate vast amounts of work, but companies lag behind because models lack institutional context—they haven't been in meetings, don't understand internal decision-making, and lack the tribal knowledge of tenured employees.
  • Organizational Ontology: To bridge this context gap, Databricks built an internal "ontology"—a massive digital graph containing millions of nodes tracking organizational relationships, resources, projects, and permissions.
  • Dogfooding Impact: Databricks uses its own ontology at scale; employees across the company (including executives and CFO Dave) query the system via the internal tool Genie rather than running down traditional reporting chains.
  • Use Case Diversity: Notable real-world AI implementations highlighted include Crisis Text Line (suicide/self-harm prevention), OmniPod (automated insulin and glucose regulation), Zipline (fully AI-driven drone delivery of blood and food), Merck's Teddy (Transformer Enhanced Drug Discovery for gene regulatory networks), and Novo Nordisk (compressing clinical trial insight generation from weeks to minutes).

Cost Management, Token Maxing, and Open Source

  • From Token Maxing to Value Maxing: Following early 2024 pushes to commit AI code directly to production, Databricks faced runaway token costs and instituted budget controls.
  • Cost-Bending Infrastructure: Databricks implemented Unit Gateway for token capacity management, budget ceilings, predictive analytics, and smart routers that deploy cheaper models or alternative harnesses for simple tasks.
  • Harness Variance: Ali Ghodsi notes that changing the harness while using the exact same model version can result in a 2x cost difference.
  • Open Source Token Dominance: While open source accounts for roughly 5% of AI spend by dollar, it drives over 60% of token volume (and up to 90% for external product applications).

Developer Tools, Databases, and Agents

  • Agentic Database Shifts: Neon and Lakebase emerged as the preferred Postgres database choice for AI agents, capturing over 90% of database creations generated by agents rather than humans.
  • Agent-First Design: Neon won agent adoption by obsessing over sub-second response times, petabyte database cloning in under a second, and a lightweight branching feature tailored for agent experimentation without runaway costs.
  • Cybersecurity Market Convergence: Ali Ghodsi argues that data/AI and cybersecurity markets are collapsing into one, necessitating automated SOC (Security Operations Center) tools like Databricks’ LakeWatch detection product to handle high-frequency attacks.

Notable Quotes

"Leaders have a responsibility to not freak people out unnecessarily unless there's really, really good reason... talking about these kind of existential risks and scenarios where all of humanity is going to be wiped out, I think it's irresponsible." — Ali Ghodsi
"The time from a CVE, vulnerability being sort of published until you see it actually be weaponized in the industry, would be like 2, 3 years... Now... it's down to like, basically hours." — Ali Ghodsi
"Models are smart enough, but they just don't have the context that exists inside of any organization. Like they have not been in every meeting, they don't know what's in everybody's heads." — Ali Ghodsi

Let's say if you map out the next 12 months, how do the enterprises actually get value? You dropped the word context, but how do they operationalize that? It's actually harder than most people believe. First and foremost, we have to make sure that we have digitized everything that's happening in an organization. You cannot just have a magic wand and make that happen. Every meeting has to be transcribed; you have to be able to get all the context of all the meetings and everything that's happening. All that digital content has to be fed to the AI. You have to build an ontology, but first and foremost, you have to collect the data. That itself is a problem in any organization because legal teams will say, "Don't record every call." So you have to do that in a way where—well, you need to define ontology for everyone. I know Palantir says the word a lot, but they don't own the word ontology. What does that mean for the people listening? How should they think about that? Ontology just means that in an organization, you define the relationship between all the abstract concepts—the goals, the departments, the people, and the projects. What do they exactly mean and what's the relationship between the people, the resources, and what that company does? It's the difference between a new employee who started today and a person who has worked there for five years. They could be equally skilled, equally smart, and equally hard-working, but the one who has been there five years has an ontology of how the organization works. They know who the people are, how you get stuff done, who to ask for help, and who won't get anything done. They know you don't need to file that specific paperwork, and they understand the history of the projects. There is a lot of ingrained knowledge sitting in everybody's heads about how an organization works. That's why in startup land they say if you lose most of your people, the company can't recover. You can't just replenish; the people are essential. How do we get that context? That's the ontology—you give that to the AI. Part of that is having the recordings, but the second part is distilling it down into a digital graph that you can then feed to the AI. The way a lot of the agents work today, like Codex or Pi or the whole slew of them, is they have this agentic loop. They can reason, but they have to check every resource one at a time. It will go to an MCP server to see if the answer is there, then it synthesizes it and gives you an answer. It's kind of slow. I liken this to if Google had built search this way 25 years ago. We would have searched for key terms, but instead of getting 10 blue links, it would have gone to one website, summarized what it does, found a few hyperlinks, jumped in parallel to a few more, read those for 10 minutes, and then given you its best 10 blue links. That would be expensive, take a long time, and the quality would be bad because you're only looking at a small subset of what exists out there. Google has an index; you never leave their servers. You search, it hits the index, and the reverse index immediately gets you the results in less than 100 milliseconds. We need to do the same thing for the AI. The ontology is that index. We need to compute it offline all the time. It's almost like the PageRank algorithm Google invented, but more complicated because there are permissions involved. The data I'm allowed to access might not be the data you're allowed to access. There is privacy and access control, and there are many different types of objects, not just websites. The problem is a little bit harder, but it's manageable. I'm convinced you can do this and get massive productivity gains, because we did it for Databricks. We did it for ourselves and the company's just completely changed. It's not the way it was a year ago because of this. You've been dogfooding Databricks forever, but maybe say more about the impact you've seen as an organization. Once we got this ontology and started working on it, our ontology became bigger than any of our customers' because Databricks uses Databricks more than anyone else. It's millions and millions of nodes in the graph. In a tree-structured organization, information flows up and down. If you can't make a decision, you escalate to your boss. Maybe they can tie-break; it escalates up. They get up to speed on the context and then make decisions. Then, those decisions have to percolate back down. A lot of this can now be done by AI if you have an ontology. In a meeting, someone has done the analysis, and they have a PowerPoint deck with graphs. That person used Excel and made some models. A lot of that you can now just do with AI. The AI can do the analysis for you, it has the context, and it can present it in a way that you want. You can ask questions about it directly instead of having follow-up meetings. It's very similar to what Jack Dorsey has said you can do to an organization; it's just a concrete way of implementing it. It's a game-changer for us. Everybody is on their phones in meetings on Genie, asking questions. You can see as soon as someone says something complicated, everybody goes to their phone. Can you share the finance anecdote you mentioned once at a board meeting? I needed to know how many customers we have in the Fortune 500 and what our penetration was. I asked someone in sales ops because I thought she would have it. She texted me back and said, "Sorry, I can't log in to Genie right now, I'm on a flight." I said, "Well, if you're just going to log into Genie, I can do that myself. I asked you because I thought you had something authoritative." I was a little angry, so I texted the CFO, Dave, and asked, "Do you know our Fortune 500 penetration?" He just copy-pasted a screenshot of Genie back. So he also asks that. I wondered if anyone does anything novel, or if everybody is just going to Genie and asking the ontology questions. It's a game-changer, but it's not like you just press a button and you have an ontology. Palantir has done a great job of going to organizations and getting that tacit knowledge written down. We automatically take that, build the graph, and feed that into agents so we can answer questions in a way that business leaders like to see—in graphs and in an analytical way where you can interrogate the data to make decisions. It's pretty amazing. You bookmarked that developers using AI is of questionable value. I want to follow up because I feel like you guys were one of the earliest to applaud people who used AI to become more productive. Then there is this cycle of, "Oh shoot, people are being wasteful, now we need to value-max." What was your own journey on that, and how do you think about value-maxing? I'll throw in Unity Gateway here too, because managing costs is getting more important and you're helping people do that. Around Q4 last year, the models got really good and we noticed it was starting to give much better productivity. I started using the models myself to commit code into production for Databricks. I started pushing the organization that everyone needs to do that. If the CEO can commit code to production on a sensitive data platform with security requirements, you should be able to do that too. We started making leaderboards. By January and February, we were already in full swing with everyone using it. But the whole "token-maxing" thing was already happening by February or March. We had the luck of being a few quarters ahead. We already had a gateway called Unity Gateway where we provided token capacity—OpenAI, Anthropic, Gemini, Groq, and open-source models. We started putting budget constraints in place, giving people warnings when they were getting close to their ceilings. We started doing great analytics so we could predict exactly where the costs were going. We added smart routers that could pick cheaper models for simple questions. We also built a harness called Omnigent which can multiplex between different harnesses. It turns out the harness itself matters; you can use the same model but different harnesses, and there is almost a 2x cost difference. If you can change the harness, you can get a lot of leverage on costs. We started using all of this, and that's how we were able to bend the curve. Our AI costs have been stagnant while token usage continues to go up. There's huge demand for this; every organization is going through this now. For the first time, in a board meeting last week, a company at scale said they're moving from the frontier models to GLM. Is that a trend, or just a one-off? I've been hearing about DeepSeek, the Kimi moment, and the next DeepSeek, but none of it seemed to have an impact on the market until now. The anecdotes I have now are pretty real. People want both. They want the latest model that's super intelligent for difficult tasks where they get ROI, but there are a lot of mundane, dumb things. People literally use their harness to rename files and what not. You're paying orders of magnitude more for that. Please type that in yourself! Don't have the model do that; it's going to spin for five minutes and rename the file for you and cost you cents. People are moving on it, but the pattern is either the expert pattern—where you have a small, cheaper open-source model that uses an expert model—or multiplexing harnesses. I think there's going to be a multitude of these. The models are stochastic and they're changing so much, so there's a lot of experimentation. We're going to get to a world where you're not always using the smartest model for everything, which has been the paradigm for the last couple of years. I see people using Fable and Astra for architecture, a cheap model for implementation, and then Fable or Astra for audit. By token count, open source is over 60%, even if by dollar it's only 5%. On the product side, startups are almost at 90% open source. Internally, a lot of them say, "We don't care, we'll just use frontier," but as they get bigger, they start bringing that down for waste reasons. I remember talking to you in 2023 when you bought Mosaic. This vision you had back then has kind of come true in 2026. Do you feel like you were early? We were pre-training for people, which doesn't make sense because there are so many good pre-trained models they can use. But you can do post-training and reinforcement learning. We're doing that at scale, and many startups are customers. If you have a repetitive task and you have a product that does something specific, it makes sense to take a really good open-source model and use reinforcement learning to make it really good at that task. You can cut costs and control your own IP. But large enterprises just need basic automation, and it's too much for them to do this right now. One challenge is that you need good Evals, and making good Evals is hard. While startups are motivated to do that, for other organizations, the "easy button" is just to use a frontier model. We actually generated Evals for customers automatically in the product and had them front and center, but people didn't want to use them. So we moved them to the back end, and they still never go to them. It's the same curse as test-driven development; everyone says it's the right way to do it, but nobody in practice does it. We've had these FDs—field data models—and the demand has gone up significantly. A lot of it is, "How do we build that ontology?" If you aren't recording anything, you can't build it. We help with that, and with building agents that are customer-facing with low latency and guardrails. For example, the Sports AI that Fox has. You can chat with it about sports, and if you try to ask it about politics, it's very good at rejecting you and moving back to sports. The FDs built that. It's important because many organizations do not have the in-house expertise to build this stuff. I saw recently that Neon was the Postgres database of choice for agents. I wouldn't have guessed that a year ago. A lot of credit goes to Nikita and the team; they've been obsessive about how to make agents favor Neon. Agents need to experiment and they need a database to come up quickly, so they had an obsession that everything should take far less than a second. You can clone a petabyte-sized database in less than a second. They built a killer feature called branching that lets you have many branches of the same database. They made it lightweight, blazing fast, and fail-safe for agents. They changed the game by focusing on agents as a persona instead of just DBAs or app developers. Now, over 90% of the databases created on Neon are created by agents. What is your P(Doom)? Less than 10%. Close to zero. My only answer is: my P(Doom) without AI is much higher than my P(Doom) with AI. How's that? That's a double. I would agree with that one.

Jared Isaacman: A New Era for NASA and American Space Exploration

All-In Podcast(1.1M)·38:34·166.8K views·2026-09-18
Jared IsaacmanCommander, Polaris Dawn Chamath PalihapitiyaHost, All-In Podcast Jason CalacanisHost, All-In Podcast
TL;DRNASA Administrator Jared Isaacman outlines a reinvigorated American space strategy centered on returning to the Moon's South Pole, developing nuclear space propulsion, and beating China in Space Race 2.0.
Internal Restructuring: NASA has shifted away from spreading resources...

TL;DR

NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman outlines a reinvigorated American space strategy centered on returning to the Moon's South Pole, developing nuclear space propulsion, and beating China in Space Race 2.0.

NASA Reform & Operational Strategy

  • Internal Restructuring: NASA has shifted away from spreading resources thin across every congressional district or trying to make everyone happy through external partnerships.
  • Core Competencies: The agency is pivoting away from rented, outsourced, or lost capabilities and stopping the reliance on a "dream state as a service" mindset.
  • Capital Allocation: NASA operates on a $25 billion budget, which Isaacman describes as sufficient when properly allocated toward high-impact missions rather than subsidizing old prime contractor technologies.
  • Commercial Leverage: NASA treats commercial space companies as partners for proven markets (launch, observation, communication) while focusing internal resources on missions with no immediate business case. SpaceX is cited as the agency's most important launch partner.

Lunar Exploration & The Artemis Campaign

  • Artemis Status: Artemis 2 successfully launched four astronauts (recipients of the Congressional Space Medal of Honor) past the Moon and back. Artemis 3 is currently being assembled, with a tanking test scheduled at Launch Complex 39B before year-end.
  • The 2027-2028 Schedule: In summer 2027, Artemis 3 will launch on the Space Launch System (SLS) into low Earth orbit to rendezvous with lander test vehicles from Blue Origin and SpaceX. In 2028, Artemis 4 will return American astronauts to the lunar surface to stay.
  • Lunar South Pole: Operations are focused on the Lunar South Pole (compared in size to Washington D.C.) because of permanently shaded regions containing water ice and crater cliffs offering near-eternal solar power. Parking spots and landing sites are extremely limited.
  • Lunar Economy & Base: NASA plans a near-monthly cadence of missions to build humanity's first permanent moon base, testing power, habitability, autonomous mobility, and in-situ resource utilization.

The Nuclear NASA & Deep Space Missions

  • Nuclear Propulsion Leap: NASA is moving away from decades of failed nuclear programs to develop nuclear-powered spacecraft, which provide high-temperature materials, efficient power conversion, reduced radiator mass, and high-performance electric propulsion (such as Hall or Ion thrusters using krypton or xenon).
  • Flagship Science & Telescopes:
    • *Dragonfly Octocopter:* Powered by an MMRTG, it will journey to Saturn's moon Titan in 2028.
    • *Europa Clipper:* Scheduled to arrive at Jupiter's icy moon in 2030.
    • *Nancy Grace Roman Space Telescope:* Equipped with a ~300-megapixel wide-field instrument and JPL-built coronagraph to study dark energy, dark matter, and hidden worlds.
    • *NEO Surveyor:* Dedicated to finding threatening asteroids and comets.

Geopolitical Competition: Space Race 2.0

  • China and Russia: China operates 25,000 miles of high-speed rail and is targeting an Apollo 11-like moon landing by 2030, collaborating with Russia on a fission-powered Moon base at the South Pole.
  • Strategic Urgency: Isaacman emphasizes that if America fails to return to the Moon after spending over $100 billion, allies and adversaries will notice, impacting global technology standards, security guarantees, and future alignment.

Aeronautics, Workforce, & The Space Academy

  • Aeronautics (First 'A' in NASA): NASA is rebuilding its X-plane fleet—starting with the X-59 quiet supersonic flight research—to return to radical airframe and engine design rather than subsidizing minor fuel-efficiency upgrades for old engines.
  • Talent & Recruitment: NASA receives overwhelming interest (taking only 1% of intern applications for its pathway program) but retains talent by focusing on "near-impossible" missions.
  • United Space Academy: President Trump established the Commission for the United States Space Academy, a NASA-led federal academy designed to train future astronauts, scientists, engineers, and space leaders.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Jared Isaacman states that Artemis 3 will launch on SLS in the summer of 2027.
  • Jared Isaacman states that American astronauts will return to the lunar surface to stay on Artemis 4 in 2028.
  • Jared Isaacman states that NASA will launch SR-1 Freedom—a 100-kilowatt fission reactor—in 2028.
  • Jared Isaacman states that the SR-1 mission will transit Mars and release SkyFall, carrying three Ingenuity-class helicopters with ground-penetrating radar.
  • Jared Isaacman states that the Dragonfly octocopter will journey to Saturn's moon Titan in 2028.
  • Jared Isaacman states that Europa Clipper will arrive at Jupiter's icy moon in 2030.
  • Jared Isaacman states that China intends to put its astronauts on the moon by 2030.

— *Jared Isaacman*

Ignition sequence start. All engines up. It's good to have an aviator and astronaut in charge. Jared Isaacman, the new NASA administrator. NASA is still hot. Humankind will not be contained to planet Earth indefinitely. In the next giant leap capabilities, that's nuclear power and propulsion; that's what extends America's reach farther into the solar system. That's what guarantees the third race will never be in question. Please welcome Jared Isaacman. Good morning, everyone. It is an absolute honor to be here at All-In. We are living through an extraordinary moment in history, aren't we? It took just 65 years from Orville and Wilbur's first flight to Neil and Buzz walking on the surface of the moon. Now, it's been 57 years since Apollo 11, and for a while there, the pace of progress hasn't been all that inspiring. But look at what is on the horizon right now. We got artificial intelligence, quantum technologies, robotics, additive manufacturing, the promise of fusion energy, biotech to heal the disabled, autonomous transportation, and this whole abundance thing everyone's talking about. All these technologies converging in the years ahead is beyond what most people can comprehend. Our world will fundamentally change: how we live, how we work, how we fight wars, and how we reach out and touch the stars. We are not alone on this journey. We are living through a great power competition. Other nations understand the opportunities that can elevate nations and change civilizations. And perhaps nowhere are the possibilities, the competition, and the consequences of getting it wrong more apparent than in the ultimate high ground above us. Now at NASA, we are doing things differently, and we are regaining our swagger and putting wins on the board. But absent this president, absent this geopolitical competition, and the very real possibility of losing the second space race, I suspect very little would have changed. For too long, resources at the world's most accomplished space agency were spread everywhere, trying to make everyone happy. Much of it was through external imposition, but plenty of it was self-inflicted. We partnered for the sake of partnerships, oftentimes becoming a drag on the mission instead of accelerating it. As a result, we have the Orion spacecraft that we can't inject into low lunar orbit like we did during Apollo. And the prior administration canceled a Mars sample return mission that was on track to cost more than an aircraft carrier. We created programs that were too big to fail and too costly to truly succeed, in the hope that they would survive administrations. The results are a rocket that was designed when China was predominantly operating coal-fired locomotives, now becoming operational as China operates 25,000 miles of high-speed rail and is just a few years away from their own Apollo 11-like moment. Core competencies and tens of thousands of the NASA workforce were rented, outsourced, or lost, which turned what should have been months worth of progress into years at a tremendously greater cost. Eventually, too little was left to do things the right way—the way NASA showed the world how to do it in decades past—so we invented this whole "dream state as a service" thing, foregoing the playbook of success and shifting the impossible burden onto others. The result is that our moon rocket is, in fact, less efficient than Saturn 5 at converting launch mass into payload headed for the moon, with more time between the Artemis 1 and Artemis 2 missions than all 12 of the Gemini missions that were flown 60 years before. A lunar space station that, whenever it may have been delivered, put our astronauts in a position to look down on the most desirable lunar real estate instead of operating on the surface and occupying it. One X-plane that wasn't flying very much, and billions spent on failed nuclear programs that had not left the laboratory since 1965. Now, during the first space race, we were slow out of the gate, but NASA ultimately ran up the score. The second will be much closer than it ever needed to be. Since becoming the administrator of NASA last year, we have made a different choice. We are not going to try and make everyone happy. We are not going to spread every penny across every district or partner with every nation just to try and make everyone happy, or because that's how people incorrectly believed it always was. I am certainly not here for the money, to favor companies, for the title, the notoriety, or the politics. I'm not here to be your VC, to entertain your dream, or invent new markets if it detracts in the slightest way from the missions that we have been entrusted to achieve on behalf of the American people. You can take those conversations up with the Department of Commerce if you like. I'm here to execute on President Trump's national space policy: to align and focus resources, to work alongside and unleash the best this nation has to offer—from the NASA workforce to our industry to our partners—and deliver the world-changing outcomes. The kind of outcomes that put Neil and Buzz on the moon; the kind of outcomes that inspired many of you to be in this room in the first place. This is a very reinvigorated and energized NASA. And America is back in the business of sending astronauts to the moon. Now, Artemis 2 was just the beginning. Those four heroic astronauts, recent recipients of the Congressional Space Medal of Honor, rode 8.8 million pounds of thrust to near-Earth escape velocities and traveled farther into space than any humans in history—around the moon and back home safely. And that was just the opening act. We are not waiting three years to fly again. We are not turning every rocket into a work of art. Artemis 3 is already being assembled right now at a pace many doubted was possible just months ago. Before year-end, we intend to roll out to launch complex 39B for a tanking test and send a message to our workforce, our industry, and our rivals overseas. NASA is back, and we are not going to sit idly by. We are not just a procurement organization. We are going to do the extremely demanding work and achieve our objectives safely, responsibly, and urgently, because that is what meeting the moment requires. Now, in the summer of 2027, Artemis 3 will launch on SLS into low Earth orbit and rendezvous with lander test vehicles from Blue Origin and SpaceX, in what will be a remarkable display of the three most powerful rockets and spacecraft in the world. We will test interoperability and show what a future multi-launch campaign can actually look like. What we learn will inform the uncrewed test landings that follow, and then Artemis 4 in 2028, when American astronauts return to the lunar surface—and this time, to stay. In parallel, we are establishing humanity's first outpost on another world: a moon base. And this time, we are leveraging the NASA playbook of decades past. We are not jumping directly to the dream state. We will launch missions on a near-monthly cadence and undertake the science of survival. That means autonomous and crewed mobility, surface improvement, in-situ resource utilization and manufacturing, logistics, habitability, power, communications, and all the science instruments the mass budget affords. We will bring it to you live and HD on the moonbase website, and we will leave no doubters this time. We go for the scientific and the economic potential. We go to learn about the formation of our solar system, but primarily because the lunar South Pole, where the water ice is, is going to be the technological proving ground for where we inevitably go next, which is Mars. In 2028, NASA will leave decades and billions of dollars of failed nuclear programs behind and launch SR-1 Freedom, a 100-kilowatt fission reactor that will finally get America underway on nuclear power. The mission will transit Mars and release SkyFall, which carries three Ingenuity-class helicopters, using ground-penetrating radar to scout subsurface ice and future landing sites. This will mark the beginning of the nuclear NASA, pivoting our workforce and facilities back to doing the near-impossible missions with no obvious business case—what no company, no agency, or nation is presently capable of accomplishing—but that extend humanity's reach farther into the outer solar system. And just like during the Apollo era, the technology that we pioneered to get there will surely benefit life back here at home. There will be lots of nuclear missions: SR-2, SR-3, SR-4. Alongside our industry partners, we will push the boundaries of high-temperature materials, more efficient power conversion, reduced radiator mass, and higher-performance electric propulsion as we visit some of the most interesting moons like Enceladus, Europa, and Titan. These are worlds with oceans, with complex chemistry and perhaps the ingredients for life—a reminder that some of the greatest discoveries in human history may be waiting for us in our own backyard. We could answer the question: are we alone, and are we alone even in our own solar system, let alone the galaxy and universe around us? Someday, a chemically augmented nuclear power transfer vehicle, part of an American star fleet supported by our armada of starships and other spacecraft, will carry humans to the surface of Mars and bring them home safely to tell us about it, and not just once. We are on this great destiny of human exploration, and we are not turning back. Along the way, we are going to do the other things. Commercial satellites are being printed off at a rate that will help us affordably understand the only planet that we presently inhabit, our home star, and space weather. We can better predict weather and perhaps respond to wildfires and natural disasters, freeing up more resources to build the exquisite flagship science missions that only NASA can undertake. Like, for example, the nuclear-powered Dragonfly octocopter. It's powered by a 2-kilowatt MMRTG converting to just 100 watts of electricity—barely better than an old light bulb—but it will journey to Saturn's moon of Titan in 2028. Europa Clipper will arrive at Jupiter's icy moon in 2030. And our great space telescopes like James Webb and Hubble will soon be joined by Roman, with her nearly 300-megapixel wide-field instrument and JPL-built coronagraph. In the moments ahead, Roman will open her eyes and in that instance, she will see more of the universe than any scientific instrument we've ever put in space before. It is a cool telescope. Her surveys will seek to understand the mysteries of dark energy and dark matter and reveal tens of thousands of worlds that are hidden behind distant stars. Roman will return images so large and in such detail, there is no screen on Earth large enough to display them. Now other missions like Neo Surveyor will find asteroids and comets that can threaten Earth, while next-generation telescopes in development will seek out habitable planets orbiting other stars. The last few decades have shown us that the future in space that we all imagined as children will never be realized if they're perpetually funded by taxpayers. NASA will do everything within reason to support an orbital and perhaps even lunar economy someday, building on the proven markets of launch, observation, and communication. Whether the next frontier is in orbital data centers, commercial space stations, on-orbit manufacturing, regolith resource extraction, asteroid mining, or industries that none of us have even imagined yet. Now, it's not NASA's job to force an economy, but we will do all we can to ignite one as we pursue our missions. And in the service of the first "A" in NASA, we are rebuilding our X-plane fleet. The X-59 is researching quiet supersonic flight, but it's just the beginning as NASA recommits to flight tests and works alongside industry to push the boundaries of airframe and propulsion design. It will not be long before NASA is flying once again as high and as fast as we have from decades past, and then even more. But if this frontier is going to expand as rapidly as we believe it will be, we will have to cultivate the talent to lead it. The space domain deserves an institution focused on that future, and call it what many already have and should: a Starfleet Academy. That is why the president established the Commission for the United States Space Academy, a NASA-led federal academy to prepare the next generation of astronauts, scientists, engineers, technicians, operators, pilots, and leaders. And just as the need for a Space Force became clear as the domain evolved, we should be equally forward-looking about preparing those who will build the moon base, operate nuclear-powered spacecraft, command missions to Mars, ensure our national security, and create industries in orbit we can barely imagine today. And there is no time to waste. I want you all to think about where you were when Artemis 2 astronauts sent back those images from the moon. Who did it touch? Your parents, your friends, your colleagues, your children. Now I want you to imagine astronauts climbing down the ladder. Only this time, it is not grainy black and white footage from July 20th, 1969. It's high definition color, streaming live to billions of people all around the world. The astronaut steps foot on the lunar surface, the camera pans up, and the flag on the spacesuit is not American. There will be no footnote explaining that we spent more, no disclaimer that our architecture was complicated, no one will care how many studies we completed, how many meetings we held, what congressional districts benefited, who all the all-star lobbyists were for their hardware, who served on the committee, or how many times we slipped the schedule for what someone thought was a perfectly reasonable reason. The world will just see who got there. China intends to put their astronauts on the moon by 2030; its robotic missions are targeting the Shackleton crater of the lunar South Pole next year, and there are only so many good parking spots in that neighborhood. And they intend to occupy them. They are working with Russia on their own nuclear-powered moon base, and to be clear, China will accomplish what the Soviets never could during the first space race. They have a very achievable two-launch architecture, the national will, and capabilities to put their astronauts on the surface of the moon, and if America has not returned despite the decades of promises and the more than 100 billion invested, the shock wave will be felt around the world. Our allies will notice, our adversaries will notice, every nation deciding whose technology to buy, whose standards to adopt, whose security guarantees to trust, and whose vision of the future to follow will take notice. And perhaps most importantly, our children will notice. That is why we must remain focused on the objectives that matter, why NASA was established in the first place; there is no time anymore for lobbying against America's interests or further tolerating the status quo. Only extreme ownership, competence, and action. Those of us inspired by the pioneers and heroes of decades past know they set the bar high, but we do not honor them by living forever on what they accomplished. This is our time to pick up where they left off, return and never give up the moon again, and then set our sights on Mars and beyond. And none of this will be accomplished by NASA alone. We have the support of President Trump, we have Congress, we have a clear mandate in the national space policy, but it will take brilliant entrepreneurs, scientists, engineers, our allies, and Americans across the country who still believe that great nations can do great things, because our children—why, they inherit the confidence of a nation still capable of the extraordinary or the memory of one that used to be. That responsibility, that choice, belongs to all of us. And I believe when history looks back on this moment, let it record that America did not hesitate any longer; we did not allow the bureaucracy, the complacency, the waste, inaction, or fear of failure to constrain what we could accomplish. The hard part is it's really challenging to do that under one atmosphere and 1G here on Earth. Can you explain just really briefly a primer on how nuclear works for generating thrust? Yeah, sure. You can just think of a lot of the satellites, like say Starlink satellites, that you'd have up right now that use Hall or Ion thrusters. They're generating electricity through solar power and then they're using electromagnetic forces to ionize either krypton or xenon and then just basically accelerating it out of the thruster, which gives you very high exhaust velocity, so it's extremely efficient, very low thrust, and you can use that propellant for a very long time. And in space, the faster the molecule shoots out the back, the more thrust it generates for the craft to move. It's very low mass flow but highly efficient, right? So very, very efficient, high exhaust velocities. The idea though is where that breaks down is the farther you get away from the sun. Once you get out towards Jupiter, solar effectiveness is negligible. So what you're using is the thermal energy from a nuclear reactor—100 kW, we'll scale it up to 250, who knows, maybe megawatt class. You're going to want to run that reactor as hot as you possibly can, so that's your high-temperature materials, and then you're going to convert it through like a closed Brayton cycle power conversion unit into electricity. And that electricity is then going to power those same thrusters that you would see on Starlinks. It's just they're scaled up; they're like 12 kW, then 14 kW, 25 kW thrusters.
