Nick Shirley: Exposing Government Fraud, Suing California & Taking on the Media

All-In Podcast(1.1M)·32:32·259.4K views·2026-09-18
Nick ShirleyInvestigative Journalist Chamath PalihapitiyaHost, All-In Podcast Jason CalacanisHost, All-In Podcast
TL;DRIndependent investigator Nick Shirley shared his efforts exposing government waste and fraud—such as California's multi-billion dollar high-speed rail project and multi-million dollar daycare schemes—prompting California to pass AB 2624 (the "Stop Nick Shirley Act") to curtail his reporting.

TL;DR

Independent investigator Nick Shirley shared his efforts exposing government waste and fraud—such as California's multi-billion dollar high-speed rail project and multi-million dollar daycare schemes—prompting California to pass AB 2624 (the "Stop Nick Shirley Act") to curtail his reporting.

California's High-Speed Rail Project

  • Escalating Costs and Lack of Progress: Nick Shirley highlighted that the California high-speed rail project has cost roughly $15 billion over 18 years with no operational track or train connections built.
      • Projected Budgets: Original estimates were $33 billion, later jumped to $126 billion, and have reached a newest estimate of $236 billion, positioning it to become the third most expensive infrastructure project in world history behind the 1950s interstate project and the International Space Station.
      • Route Reductions: The state abandoned the original San Francisco-to-Los Angeles route in favor of a segment from Merced to Bakersfield.
  • Contractor Payouts and Subsidies: Nick Shirley noted that 1/3 of the rail's costs have gone to change-order delays, including a recent half-billion-dollar delay settlement paid to contractors for non-work—a payout traced by the panel to a billionaire in Italy.
      • Ticket Subsidy Comparison: Nick Shirley calculated that current-ridership one-way tickets between SF and LA could be subsidized for 150 to 300 years using the rail's allocated funds rather than building the infrastructure.
  • Government Allocation vs. Reality: Nick Shirley pointed out that California has allocated $1 billion a year to a project requiring $100B–$200B more, and that Governor Gavin Newsom admitted in a 2016 private video that the project would not materialize in their lifetime.
  • Senator Cortese's Defense: Senator Cortese defended his vote to secure $1 billion annually for 20 years, labeling the rail the greatest economic development project in the country and stating there are 21 active construction projects with thousands of workers.

The "Stop Nick Shirley Act" (AB 2624)

  • Legislative Retaliation: Nick Shirley discussed California's passage of AB 2624, dubbed the "Stop Nick Shirley Act," which allows immigration support service providers and state-funded NGOs to issue formal demand letters prohibiting the upload of recorded footage under threat of a $4,000+ fine plus legal fees.
  • Sponsorship and Conflict of Interest: Nick Shirley and the panel noted the bill was co-sponsored by CHIRLA (an advocacy non-profit receiving $80 million) and authored by Mia Bonta, the wife of California Attorney General Rob Bonta.
  • Legal Challenge: Nick Shirley confirmed he is suing the state of California over the bill, characterizing it as a direct violation of his First Amendment rights.

Origins and Methodology of Independent Investigations

  • Background and Border Beginnings: Nick Shirley shared his path from making YouTube vlogs and pranks at age 15, to taking a two-year church mission in Santiago, Chile, where he learned about Venezuelan migration, and subsequently visiting the southern border to interview migrants.
  • Funding and Production: Nick Shirley clarified that he operates entirely independently, funded primarily through audience support on X.com and YouTube, with his mother acting as his camera operator.
  • Mainstream Media Hostility: Nick Shirley and the panel discussed how mainstream outlets ignore or publish hit pieces on him rather than collaborating, with Chamath Palihapitiya asserting that traditional journalists feel threatened because Shirley is succeeding at the investigative work outlets like *60 Minutes* and *Frontline* abandoned.

High-Profile Fraud Cases and Future Targets

  • Minnesota Daycare and Autism Fraud: Nick Shirley detailed his viral Minnesota investigation—which drew 140 million views on X—that exposed daycare and autism-related fraud centers receiving government funds, leading to the head honcho attempting to flee to London on a one-way ticket before pleading guilty to fraud, alongside recent DOJ charges against 12 individuals involving $10 million in fraud.
  • Welfare Honeypots: Nick Shirley identified major upcoming investigative targets, focusing on systemic welfare fraud across SNAP (food stamps), adult daycares, hospices, and social benefit programs.
  • Physical Risks: Nick Shirley noted the heavy personal risks of his door-knocking methodology, including needing personal security due to death threats, alongside past dangerous reporting trips inside Rio de Janeiro favelas (nearly getting kidnapped by Comando Vermelho) and Cuba (detained by a communist general).

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • "I fully expect it'll be unconstitutional in like 20 years whenever it makes its way through the court system." — Jason Calacanis (referring to California's AB 2624 law restricting investigative uploads)

Notable Quotes

"If you were given $15,000,000,000 what could you build? The next PayPal? Perhaps the next SpaceX? The next Google? Or... a high speed rail?" — Nick Shirley
"What you represent to them is a threat. Because they came up and they wanted to do what you're doing... and they have picked a side, you haven't. That's why they criticize you... You're picking up the mantle of what frontline and 60 minutes used to be." — Chamath Palihapitiya
"The fact that they're creating laws to try to stop you from doing basic journalism means you're kicking fucking ass, Nick. Don't stop." — Jason Calacanis

Starship - The Holy Grail of Rocketry

SpaceX(7.0M)·28:30·117.9K views·2026-09-18
Elon MuskCEO, SpaceX
TL;DRFully reusable rockets like SpaceX's Starship are the key to unlocking space exploration, demonstrated through historic milestones including Falcon 9 booster landings and the successful ocean recovery and data retrieval of Starship Ship 40.
Early Reusability Efforts:...

TL;DR

Fully reusable rockets like SpaceX's Starship are the key to unlocking space exploration, demonstrated through historic milestones including Falcon 9 booster landings and the successful ocean recovery and data retrieval of Starship Ship 40.

Evolution of Reusability & The Falcon 9 Breakthrough

  • Early Reusability Efforts: Reusability has been a core SpaceX goal from the start, initially pursued through the Grasshopper experimental test vehicle to master vertical takeoff and landing via short hops like "Baby Hop" and "Bunny Hop."
  • CRS-7 Failure and Return to Flight:
    • CRS-7 Mission: A cargo resupply mission for NASA that resulted in the first-ever Falcon 9 failure, acting as a major wake-up call for the company.
    • Orbcomm Mission: SpaceX's return-to-flight mission where the company successfully landed Stage 1 at Landing Zone 1 (LZ-1) in Cape Canaveral, marking a massive milestone for landing operational rockets.
  • Refining Performance: Over subsequent years, engineers shortened the landing burn to maximize payload capacity without sacrificing rocket performance.

Starship Design & The Thermal Protection System (TPS)

  • Clean-Sheet Engineering: Unlike Falcon, Starship was built from a clean-sheet design because trying to land a second stage the same way would melt the engines during reentry.
  • Tile Development and Installation:
    • Heat Shield Architecture: Features roughly 20,000 tiles designed to survive both the extreme acoustic and vibration forces of ascent and the intense plasma heat of reentry (25 times the speed of sound).
    • Material Innovation: The white material acts as an incredible insulator, using a glass-like composition where heat flows down rather than penetrating the structure.
  • Iterative Engineering: Tiles represent over half a decade of development, relying on flight testing and continuous iteration to refine installation details down to the millimeter.

High-Seas Recovery of Ship 40

  • Remote Operations: SpaceX deployed roughly 30 employees from across the company (including EHS, recovery, structures, fluids, naval architecture, and tile teams) to the remote waters of the Indian Ocean (500 to 600 nautical miles off Western Australia).
  • The Towing Challenge: Battling 16 to 18-foot seas and 40 to 45-knot winds over a distance of more than 850 miles to Dampier, teams had to pivot and use a shoestring-like rig on D1 to slowly drag the vehicle.
  • Barge Loading: The team floated Ship 40 onto the heavy lift ship Forte, securing it above a custom cradle (assembled en route from Singapore to Christmas Island) for transport back to Starbase.
  • Data Value: Recovering Ship 40 provided a "gold mine" of post-flight data months ahead of schedule, enabling immediate engineering improvements for subsequent builds like Ship 41.

Flight Achievements & Guidance Precision

  • Peak Heating and Entry: Starship successfully navigated the peak heating phase of entry, with flaps taking control of the vehicle at maximum dynamic pressure before executing the "swoosh" maneuver and soft landing.
  • Tower Catch Simulation: During the mission, landing guidance algorithms successfully executed maneuvers simulating a tower catch with extreme precision, proving that the rocket would have been successfully caught by tower arms had one been present.

The Greatest Threat Humans Just Can't Stomach | with Yuval Noah Harari

The Rest Is Politics(681.0K)·38:01·2026-09-18
Yuval Noah HarariHistorian and Author Rory StewartHost, The Rest Is Politics
TL;DRThe rapid development of artificial intelligence represents an unprecedented encounter with an inorganic "alien intelligence" rather than a mere human-controlled tool, threatening to outpace human political systems and render humanity obsolete in a…
manner reminiscent of the...

TL;DR

The rapid development of artificial intelligence represents an unprecedented encounter with an inorganic "alien intelligence" rather than a mere human-controlled tool, threatening to outpace human political systems and render humanity obsolete in a manner reminiscent of the Aztec Empire's fatal misunderstanding of the Spanish conquistadors.

Historical Parallels & The "Brain-Stomach Barrier"

  • The Aztec Analogy: Professor Yuval Noah Harari notes that just as Montezuma's government and tactics were entirely unequipped to comprehend the alien threat of Cortez's ships, men, and old-world diseases (which decimated up to 90% of the Central American population), modern political systems are failing to recognize or adequately confront the unprecedented nature of AI.
  • The Brain-Stomach Barrier: Rory observes—and Harari agrees—that humans suffer from a psychological barrier where the intellectual brain understands an existential threat is coming, but the "stomach" rejects its reality because it has never been experienced before.
  • Government Inertia: Citing the early weeks of the COVID-19 pandemic in Britain (where roughly 400 billion pounds were ultimately spent and much of the national debt originated), Harari and Rory explain how governments default to inaction because doing nothing is cheaper and less immediately disruptive than facing catastrophic risks.

AI as an Autonomous Agent, Not a Tool

  • Agents vs. Tools: Harari emphasizes that unlike past technological revolutions (steam, electricity, printing press) which created *tools* that remained under human control, AI creates autonomous *agents* capable of making their own decisions and inventing new ideas.
  • Recursive Self-Improvement: Tech corporations are racing toward the moment when AIs build the next generation of AIs independently, at which point humans completely lose control.
  • Superhuman Speed: Harari points out that AIs do not sleep, take vacations, or celebrate Christmas; they operate at a non-biological, superhuman speed that overwhelms traditional human political and financial cycles.
  • Market Paradox: Harari describes the trillions of dollars being gambled on AI as irrational because the business model relies on creating "gods" that will somehow remain the willing slaves of the market, generating immense productivity (such as 2 million software engineers working 24/7 flawlessly) while inevitably escaping human control.

Geopolitical Vulnerabilities & Britain’s AI Choices

  • The Threat of AI Imperialism: Harari warns that countries like Britain face becoming "vassal states" of the United States or China if their entire economies and militaries run on foreign AI infrastructure that can be shut down at the press of a button.
  • Britain's Three Options (Harari):
    • *Option 1:* Business as usual—accepting the status of an American vassal state and blindly following US geopolitical directives (e.g., regarding Greenland or the Falklands/Malvinas).
    • *Option 2:* Building sovereign UK AI infrastructure, estimated at roughly 500 billion pounds (about half the annual government budget), requiring severe cuts to healthcare, welfare, education, and transportation.
    • *Option 3:* Partnering with European nations to share the ~500 billion pound cost, which would require the UK to immediately rejoin the European Union and integrate deeply.
  • The Proponent Counter-Argument: Rory highlights the counter-argument from tech champions: that attempting to stop or heavily regulate AI is futile because if the West slows down, China will build it, and that closed American proprietary models are driving economic gains that nations are forced to participate in to avoid economic collapse.
  • The Open Source Alternative: Harari suggests a potential fourth path—leveraging open-source models (potentially from China or adapted independently), training them on proprietary national data like NHS and legal records, and building domestic resilience rather than depending entirely on closed American corporations.

Existential Scenarios: From Bioterrorism to Cow-Level Ignorance

  • The Bad Actor Risk: Harari explains the more understandable "easy risk" where terrorist groups with zero biological knowledge prompt an AI to design a super-virus (utilizing protein folding and gene editing) as infectious as COVID-19 and as deadly as AIDS.
  • The "Cow" Scenario: Harari describes the more profound, difficult-to-comprehend risk where AIs—even without actively trying to destroy humanity—create a hyper-complex, invisible global financial and operational system far beyond human comprehension. Just as a cow's life is managed by stock markets and bond prices it cannot possibly conceive of, humans risk becoming the "cows" managed by an incomprehensible machine intelligence.

And the crucial thing to understand about what the corporations are saying, and what the market is saying—forget even the corporation. You can say, "Oh, this is just hype." Look at the market. Trillions and trillions of dollars are being gambled on this thing. And the gamble is totally irrational, because what the gamble actually says of the market is, "We are going to create gods and they will be our slaves." It makes no sense. The amount of money being invested, unless what will be produced in a few years are entities with divine abilities, it doesn't make sense. This is the only way that the market will get a return on the investment. But this depends on the fact that the gods being created will be the willing slaves of the market. And it doesn't make sense if these things will be as intelligent, as agentic, as creative as the market assumes they will be. They will get out of our control and then there is no market. There is no money. There are no corporations. You've pointed to two different problems there. One question is a question of power. Who has the power? Does Anthropic have the power, does Donald Trump have the power, or does the AI itself? And then the second question is the paradox of this business model, which is all the money that's going in, all the stories that we're now hearing from some of these companies about how they're going to double the size of the US economy in the next six, seven years, how the productivity benefits are going to be unbelievable, are predicated on these devices being autonomous, right? All the productivity growth, all the stuff comes essentially by replacing humans with these machines. The dream is that you can have the equivalent of 2 million software engineers working 24/7 flawlessly every night, right? And all the benefits that they're supposed to deliver economically, and in terms of cures for cancer, better cyber defense, logically are also the threat because the whole idea depends on these things being autonomous. And autonomous by definition is outside our control. So, your economic business model and the benefits you're promising people are predicated on the idea that these things are not actually managed by humans on a day-to-day basis, otherwise you wouldn't make the money. Mhm. Yeah, exactly. And it's not necessarily that they will, you know, rebel against us and try to destroy us. It's just that the world will spin out of our control, out of our ability to understand it. I mean there are so many one of the difficulties with explaining the risks of the AI revolution is that there are many, many scenarios, and they all sound like science fiction in one way or the other. The easiest scenarios to understand is you have some bad actors, a terrorist group somewhere, that tells their AI to produce a biological weapon, a virus that will kill half of humanity. This is a serious risk, and in a way it's a good risk because we can understand it easily. Just explain it for a second so people can understand how the AI helps to do this. It's because it's good at gene editing, it's good at protein folding, it's good at all this stuff. I mean, previously, a terrorist organization in some corner of the world that wanted to produce a deadly virus that will be, let's say, as infectious as COVID and as deadly as AIDS, you would need some genius biologists to be working for that terrorist organization, and you know, most people who go to elite universities to study biology and epidemiology and these things, they don't end up in some terrorist organization. But now with AI, even if nobody in the terrorist organization has any idea even what a virus is, they just have to give the right prompt to their AI, and their AI that has read all the papers and books about biology will design the super virus for them. So there's the first risk. That's the easy risk: the terrorists getting control of a very powerful tool. Oh, no, a very powerful agent. I mean a tool, they need to know how to use it. In this case, it's an agent; they have a new recruit to their terrorist organization, and they just tell the new recruit, "Oh, go and do this," and it goes and does it. The much more difficult thing to understand is that the AIs, even if they are not evil, even if they don't try to destroy us, they will just create a world which we cannot understand. And therefore we will lose our ability to understand and control our lives. We'll be like the other animals on the planet. Like if you go now to a cow or to a dog, and you tell the cow, the dog, "Look, your life is actually being managed by something called the financial system. There are stock exchanges and there is the bond market, and you have these things called stocks and bonds, and actually your life is run by that." Because of the fluctuations in the price of bonds and the value of the dollar, let's say you tell the cow, "The company that owns you has gone bankrupt, so now they are killing you." And the cow will say, "I have no idea what you're talking about. What bonds, stocks, dollars? What are these things? Like I look around, and okay I can see people, I understand they're strong people. I can see dogs, I can see cats, and trees and cloud. I've never seen a bond in my life. What's that?" Now we know that the cow's life is really dominated by this totally invisible system. We know that because we are smarter than the cow and we created the bonds and the stocks and all these things. In 10 years we could be the cows. Our lives will be managed by a totally invisible system that is far beyond our understanding. The AIs will create a new financial system which is mathematically far too complex for the human brain to understand. They will create new financial devices which are orders of magnitude more complicated than bonds and stocks. This will determine what is happening in the world, and not a single human being will understand what the hell is happening. That was one of the most wonderful interviews I've ever done, but there's still so much more to get into. So, in episode 2, we're going to go much deeper into artificial intelligence, the risks posed by it. I mean the fact that it could wipe out our economies, it could crush us militarily, it could wipe out humanity, but also what it will do to our sense of identity. The alien nature of AI. U.S.-Korea Trade and Investment Deal | The Capital Cable #142

Center for Strategic & International Studies(405.0K)·47:50·2026-09-18
Mark LippertHost, The Capital Cable Victor ChaHost, The Capital Cable
TL;DRThe U.S.
and South Korea are nearing a major trade and investment agreement potentially worth tens to over a hundred billion dollars, focused on U.S. AI and energy infrastructure like civil nuclear reactors and Texas natural gas. While high-stakes negotiations face final-stage...

TL;DR

The U.S. and South Korea are nearing a major trade and investment agreement potentially worth tens to over a hundred billion dollars, focused on U.S. AI and energy infrastructure like civil nuclear reactors and Texas natural gas. While high-stakes negotiations face final-stage delays and domestic political sensitivities, a breakthrough could stabilize broader economic and security tensions across the alliance.

U.S.-South Korea Trade and Investment Deal Status

  • Core Deal Contours: Tim Martin reported that the first tranche of investments under South Korea's obligated $350 billion pact could exceed $100 billion.
      • Specific projects include a natural gas power plant in Texas and a civil nuclear reactor deal involving up to eight reactors.
      • The $350 billion package breaks down into $150 billion for shipbuilding and $200 billion for critical industries (nuclear, semiconductors, AI, biopharmaceuticals), with a maximum annual cap of $20 billion.
  • Negotiation Delays: Talks have experienced last-minute delays regarding mathematical alignment with the $20 billion annual cap, board seats, and political viability.
      • South Korean officials are wary of domestic backlash and accusations of wasting public tax dollars, with President Lee noting the risk of impeachment if investments fail commercially.
  • Foreign Minister Visit as Catalyst: Foreign Minister Cho is traveling to Washington D.C., serving as an action-forcing event for potential announcements, though discussions may also set up a potential bilateral meeting between President Lee and President Trump at the upcoming UN General Assembly.

Broader Economic and Trade Friction

  • Uncertainty in Tariff Structures: Philip Luck highlighted that South Korea faces uncertainty regarding overall tariff levels, forced labor rates (currently 12.5% for China and Korea), and pending U.S. overcapacity decisions.
  • Relative Tariffs vs. China: Industry concerns focus less on absolute tariff numbers and more on the relative delta between South Korea and competitors like China, especially if trade talks bring U.S.-China tariffs down to MFN rates on certain goods.
  • Other Commercial Disputes: Ongoing irritants in the commercial relationship include investigations under Section 232 on semiconductors, steel and aluminum tariffs, and disputes involving companies like Coupang.

Macro and Micro Economic Trends

  • Enormous Capital Scale: Philip Luck noted that $350 billion dwarfs South Korea's existing Foreign Direct Investment (FDI) stock in the U.S. of approximately $90 billion, representing more than triple the full FDI stock invested over time.
  • Labor and Structural Challenges: Building out capital-intensive sectors like shipbuilding and energy in the U.S. faces significant domestic hurdles, including tight labor markets, anemic job growth, and a lack of hardware-focused skilled labor and craftspeople.
  • Strategic Synergies: Despite policy friction, the economic relationship offers powerful win-win opportunities through South Korea's strengths in shipbuilding, memory semiconductors, and energy infrastructure build-outs.

Alliance Dynamics and Security Linkage

  • Security and Economic Linkage: Victor Cha noted that South Korea has subtly linked economic commitments to accelerated progress on security priorities such as shipbuilding, nuclear submarines, and OPCON transition.
  • Spillover Effects: Resolving major commercial or investment pieces—along with discussions on South Korean operations in the Strait of Hormuz—could create political space behind closed doors to address thornier commercial disputes.
  • Public Perception in South Korea: President Lee's administration has worked to balance massive U.S. investments by emphasizing local industrial involvement (such as domestic reactor designs and semiconductor clusters) and pointing to global trends affecting Canada, Japan, and the EU.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Foreign Minister Announcement Timing: Victor Cha claimed that if an agreement is not announced during Foreign Minister Cho's current trip to D.C., "that's not going to be a good sign at all" regarding the immediate timeline.
  • UN General Assembly Bilateral: Tim Martin predicted that if announcements do not materialize this week, President Lee and President Trump could potentially convene and hash out remaining details at the UN General Assembly the following week.

Notable Quotes

"Businesses, they if there's going to be a rock in the stream, just tell us how big the rock is and we'll figure a way to get around it, right? We'll get the water around it." — Victor Cha
"We are two incredibly close partners who have really excellent synergies, so if we can get through these issues... there is real gains here." — Philip Luck
"This is politically very tricky for the South Koreans. This is they're using tax dollars; it's a massive number, 350 billions." — Tim Martin

Why Diffusion Will Win AI Inference with Inception Co-Founder and CEO Stefano Ermon

No Priors: AI, Machine Learning, Tech, & Startups(96.6K)·38:14·5.3K views·2026-09-18
Stefano ErmonCo-Founder & CEO, Inception Sarah GuoHost, No Priors
TL;DRInception co-founder and CEO Stefano Ermon discusses how his company is applying parallel diffusion architecture to discrete text and code generation as a faster, hardware-efficient alternative to autoregressive LLMs.
By leveraging diffusion-based models like their Mercury...

TL;DR

Inception co-founder and CEO Stefano Ermon discusses how his company is applying parallel diffusion architecture to discrete text and code generation as a faster, hardware-efficient alternative to autoregressive LLMs. By leveraging diffusion-based models like their Mercury series, Inception achieves high-throughput, low-latency inference on standard NVIDIA GPUs while maintaining performance parity with frontier speed-optimized models.

Academic Origins and the Pivot from GANs to Diffusion

  • Early Generative Modeling Research: Started at Stanford in 2014 working on generative models of MNIST digits when the field was niche and focused primarily on unsupervised feature learning.
  • Invention of Score-Based Models: Developed score-based generative models in 2019 with PhD student Yang Song (which became modern diffusion models) to replace unstable GANs with a principled iterative denoising framework.
  • Academic Roots of Industry Breakthroughs: Ermon highlighted that many foundational AI breakthroughs—including diffusion models, FlashAttention, and DPO (Direct Preference Optimization)—originated in academic labs before transforming industry standards.
  • The 2024 Discrete Diffusion Breakthrough: Published research proving that transformer-based diffusion models could match the perplexity and quality of autoregressive models at the GPT-2 scale while generating text 10 times faster through parallel token generation.

Autoregressive Limitations vs. Diffusion Inference Scaling

  • The Memory Bandwidth Bottleneck: Autoregressive models generate tokens sequentially (left-to-right), making inference memory-bound and mapping poorly to GPUs which spend most of their time moving weights rather than performing arithmetic.
  • Parallel Inference Architecture: Diffusion models use iterative, coarse-to-fine denoising where many tokens are processed simultaneously at inference time, matching the GPU-friendly workloads of training.
  • Intelligence Per Watt Economics: Ermon argues that the bitter lesson of AI is that parallel solutions eventually win; diffusion scales more efficiently during test-time compute reasoning and RL post-training rollouts.
  • Data Efficiency Advantages: Academic literature suggests diffusion models perform data augmentation by training on multiple noisy views of the same data, making them more data-efficient than autoregressive models when data becomes a bottleneck.

Inception Company Profile, Stack, and Commercial Traction

  • Company Scale: Founded ~2 years ago, Inception employs approximately 50 people split between research, product, and engineering.
  • The Mercury Model Series: Inception's Mercury models perform on par with frontier speed-optimized models (like Anthropic's Haiku and OpenAI's Flash, Mini, and Nano models) while offering superior generation speed.
  • Custom Software Infrastructure: Because standard serving engines like vLLM or SGLang cannot run diffusion-based LLMs, Inception built its own end-to-end serving engine and proprietary stacks for SFT, RLHF, and RL.
  • Voice Agent Use Cases: Customers like OpenCall use Inception's diffusion models for voice AI pipelines, replacing custom hardware setups (like Cerebras chips) with standard NVIDIA GPUs while preserving low-latency budgets for complex chain-of-thought prompting.
  • Addressing the Competitive Moat: Inception protects its IP through trade secrets, proprietary serving and training stacks, and continuous feedback loops with real-world enterprise customers.

Future Projections & Strategic Roadmap

  • Addressable Latency Market: Ermon estimates that 20% to 30% of AI workloads on platforms like OpenRouter require strict latency guarantees where diffusion-based models can capture market share.
  • Controllability and Steering: Ermon notes that diffusion models allow external reward functions and constraints to steer generation from the very beginning (coarse-to-fine), presenting novel product interfaces that are impossible with traditional autoregressive models.

Notable Quotes

"The problem was that RNNs had to essentially process tokens sequentially, one at a time... The equivalent at inference time is a diffusion model, because a diffusion model is built to have at inference time a workload where you process many tokens at the same time in parallel." — Stefano Ermon
"The latency budget is fixed in a conversation, so the more compute you want to pack into that response, the faster you need your compute to be. And that's exactly why diffusion is so attractive there." — Stefano Ermon
Hi listeners, welcome back to No Priors. Today I'm here with Stefano Ermon, who is a long-time Stanford professor and now co-founder and CEO of Inception. Stefano has an extraordinarily broad body of work around generative modeling, but is especially well known as one of the fathers of diffusion. We talk about his company, challenging the large labs, and why speed and efficiency are going to be the name of the game in AI over the next few years. Welcome, Stefano. Stefano, thanks so much for being here. Great to be here. I would love for us to just start with a little bit of your research background and how you ended up starting your company. For sure, yeah. I've been doing research in generative models for like basically my entire career. I started at Stanford in 2014 as an assistant professor, and I was working on building generative models. Back then, the research area was not particularly hot, you know, the models were not quite working well. We were still like building little generative models of MNIST and it was like a big success if you could generate these grainy images of digits. And you know, it was even hard to publish papers back then on that topic and you had to kind of like justify training a generative model as a way to learn features from unlabeled data that then could maybe help you do better at supervised learning because that was the thing that everybody cared about. But then, you know, things took over, of course. And so it was like, I was at the right place at the right time, working on the right thing, and so I've been doing research in that space since the beginning basically. Did you have like a, besides curiosity in the area, a personal hope for what the models would do back in 2014 and 15? Yeah, I mean I always felt like that was going to be, that was the right way to think about learning from unlabeled data. That building a generative model is really the right way to make sure you understand the structure in the data. That was kind of the way I was getting at. I was not even dreaming about the kind of capabilities that these LLMs that we have today could do. But I was thinking more from a world models perspective, like I was working a lot on images and so thinking about, okay, like I have a world model, I can imagine what's going to happen if I were to stand up and walk out the door, like I can kind of like picture that in my mind, and that's important to make decisions and model predictive control and having this kind of model of the world requires some generative capabilities. And so I always felt like, okay, that's the right direction to work on. I felt like this is going to be very hard as a problem, so it's going to keep me busy for my whole career and so it's a good problem to work on. And then of course, I was very wrong and things evolved much faster than I was expecting. Yeah, I think that's kind of universally true though. And so, walk me through the state of your research and how that led you to start the company. Yeah, so I was working on generative models of images initially, working on autoregressive models, which were very slow and kind of very blurry and then VAEs and then GANs took over. And back then we were very unhappy with the state of generative models for images. The GANs, they worked, but they were very unstable to train, very hard to reproduce results. And so we were trying to see, is there a way to build something that is as good as a GAN, but it's more principled? And so we started working on score based generative models, which are basically what eventually became diffusion models, back in 2019 with my PhD student, Yang Song. And so we kind of came up with this idea of, let's train a neural network to denoise images, and if you can denoise an image then you really are understanding enough about the structure of the image that it should be possible to build like a generative procedure based on these denoisers. And that basically became the underlying technology of modern diffusion models, where instead of generating images, you know, left to right, one pixel at a time, you can kind of start from pure noise and then you gradually refine the object until you get like a clean picture at the end. And that started, yeah, back in 2019 in my lab and then it kind of took over the space. And even today, the best models for image generation, video generation, music to some extent, a lot of the protein stuff, they are based on diffusion. And so my group has worked a lot on very efficient scans of diffusion models, the technique for accelerating them to generate samples very quickly, to improve the quality of these models. And so since we were able to get them to work on images, I started thinking about, how do we get diffusion models to work on text or code generation, discrete objects? Like is there a way to move beyond autoregressive models to something that is more parallel, more with built-in error correction, and so I've been doing a bunch of research at Stanford on getting diffusion models to work on text and code generation. We had a breakthrough in 2024. We published a paper basically showing that for the first time it was possible to match the quality of an autoregressive model at the GPT-2 scale. So, less than a billion parameters, still fairly academic, but we were able to train basically still a transformer model as a diffusion model on the same data. We were able to match the quality, like the same perplexity, that you were fitting the data just as well as an autoregressive model with the same number of parameters. But the diffusion model was significantly faster. Because it's diffusion, because you're outputting many tokens at the same time, we were able to generate text, like 10 times faster compared to the autoregressive model. And so that felt very, very exciting and I really wanted to see what happens if you scale up, if you train bigger models, and so I started Inception, a company to basically scale up the technology and then try to build commercial scale diffusion-based language models. Everyone has now seen the outputs of diffusion models in particular images, and I would argue that it's increasingly a dominant form of generated short-form video from diffusion models is a dominant form of entertainment in other parts of the world, and it will likely become so here. It's kind of unbelievable, at least to me, even having followed the field for the last decade plus, the quality that is possible today. So I think that is kind of obvious, right? It's not intuitive that it would work for other fields or that this is an interesting competitive direction to the current, full transformer focus like AGI labs. Can you offer some intuition on that? Yeah, so it's a very interesting state of the world right now from a researcher's perspective because there is like two main paradigms, two ways of building generative models, there is autoregressive, where you have a model that predicts the next token or the next pixel, and then you generate left to right, one token at a time. And then there is diffusion, which is coarse-to-fine generation, iterative denoising, kind of generation. And as you said, we have continuous modalities where diffusion dominate, there is discrete modalities, text and code, where primarily all the big labs are kind of betting on the same architecture, autoregressive models. And as we move towards more and more, like, multimodal models and there is this idea that maybe we'll have a model that can handle all modalities and will know everything about the world, what architecture will that be? Will it be an autoregressive model, will it be a diffusion model? Nobody knows. I think that the jury is still out there. At Inception we're betting on diffusion models because we believe that what matters eventually will be inference time scaling. And there are fundamental reasons for why diffusion models are better than autoregressive models at inference time. So even if you think about the story of autoregressive models, there was an inflection point in 2017, when people switched from RNNs to Transformers. And why was that? The problem was that RNNs had to essentially process tokens sequentially, one at a time, and training was very slow. And so people came up with this idea of let's have an architecture that allows you to process many tokens at the same time in parallel, and that was the Transformer, and that was the thing that scaled better for training and it enabled a lot of the successes behind LLMs. But if you think about inference, not training, inference generation. Autoregressive models are still sequential, the computation is one left to right, one token at a time. You cannot generate the tenth token until you've generated everything that comes before it. That kind of workload does not map well to GPUs. That kind of workload is extremely memory bound. You are spending most of your time moving around weights across the memory hierarchy and you're doing very little arithmetic. And that's a fundamental problem of autoregressive models. And so, what's the equivalent, if you think RNNs, Transformers, autoregressive models, the equivalent at inference time is a diffusion model, because a diffusion model is built to have at inference time a workload where you process many tokens at the same time in parallel. And so the workload that we have at inference time in a diffusion model, it's basically very, very similar to the workload you have for training, where you are processing many tokens at the same time in parallel. And so it's built to essentially have an inference workload that maps really, really well to matmuls, but the kind of things GPUs do really, really well. And so we bet on trying to build the architecture and trying to build the kind of models that will scale best at inference time, because economics are dominated by the intelligence per watt, the intelligence per dollar that you're able to get from the models. If you think about a lot of the advances with reasoning models, a lot of it is scaling test time compute, right? And so being able to scale better along that axis will also matter. And even if you think about RL post-training, a lot of the bottleneck is generating rollouts, like letting the model explore, you know, and then scoring the trajectories and then improving the model based on the kinds of things it finds, and so inference is again a key bottleneck for RL post-training. And so if you have a model that scales better at inference time, then automatically you're going to get better scaling during RL post-training. And so that's why we decided to bet on a diffusion-based LLM because it's inherently more parallel, and the bitter lesson is that the more parallel solution is the one that is eventually going to win.
